=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an integer array nums.
You can do the following operation on the array at most once:

Choose any integer x such that nums remains non-empty on removing all occurrences of x.
Remove all occurrences of x from the array.

Return the maximum subarray sum across all possible resulting arrays.
A subarray is a contiguous non-empty sequence of elements within an array.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [-3,2,-2,-1,3,-2,3]
Output: 7
Explanation:
We can have the following arrays after at most one operation:

The original array is nums = [-3, 2, -2, -1, 3, -2, 3]. The maximum subarray sum is 3 + (-2) + 3 = 4.
Deleting all occurences of x = -3 results in nums = [2, -2, -1, 3, -2, 3]. The maximum subarray sum is 3 + (-2) + 3 = 4.
Deleting all occurences of x = -2 results in nums = [-3, 2, -1, 3, 3]. The maximum subarray sum is 2 + (-1) + 3 + 3 = 7.
Deleting all occurences of x = -1 results in nums = [-3, 2, -2, 3, -2, 3]. The maximum subarray sum is 3 + (-2) + 3 = 4.
Deleting all occurences of x = 3 results in nums = [-3, 2, -2, -1, -2]. The maximum subarray sum is 2.

The output is max(4, 4, 7, 4, 2) = 7.

Example 2:

Input: nums = [1,2,3,4]
Output: 10
Explanation:
It is optimal to not perform any operations.

 
Constraints:

1 <= nums.length <= 10^5
-10^6 <= nums[i] <= 10^6

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def maxSubarraySum(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        
```

- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

So we can maintain segment tree where each leaf has:
- value: current transformed value (original if alive, 0 if dead)
- alive: 1 if original value != current deleted v, 0 if equal.
When we process v, we need set all leaves with original value v to dead (alive=0, val=0). After query, restore to alive=1, val=original. For leaves with original value 0, if v=0 they become dead; if v!=0 they remain alive with val=0. So point update toggles a leaf's alive flag and value.

If we process each distinct v independently, total point updates = 2 * n (each occurrence updated to dead and restored) if we do for each v. But wait, for each distinct v, we update all its occurrences to dead, query, then restore. Total updates over all v = 2n. Yes. Complexity O(n log n) plus distinct queries. This seems too easy? But is the transformed array with dead positions as 0 and alive positions original values exactly equivalent to array after deleting all occurrences of v? Let's verify. After deleting v, the array consists of alive elements in original order, with no gaps. A subarray in the resulting array corresponds to a contiguous sequence of alive elements in original order. Good. Any contiguous transformed subarray with alive elements corresponds to a contiguous block in filtered array because dead elements are removed and alive elements between remain in order. Now, is processing each distinct value with point updates O(n log n) truly? Let's detail.

We need group positions by value. Values can be negative, up to 1e6. Use dict value -> list of indices. For each value v, positions list. We update each position i to dead:
- leaf value = 0
- leaf alive = 0
Then query root.best1. Then restore:
- leaf value = original nums[i]
- leaf alive = 1
Total updates = 2 * total occurrences = 2n. Each update O(log n). Query root O(1). Distinct values up to n. So O(n log n). Memory O(n).

But wait, if a value appears many times, updating all its occurrences to dead and then restoring all is O(k log n). Sum over values 2n. Good.

Need implement segment tree iterative or recursive? n=1e5, O(n log n) with recursive segment tree okay in Python if optimized? 2n updates = 2e5, each update O(log n ~17), so ~3.4 million node operations; recursive function calls maybe okay but need be careful. Distinct values up to 1e5, queries 1e5, but query root O(1). Updates 2e5. Recursive segment tree with arrays size 4n, update function recursive ~17 calls per update => 3.4M calls, okay in Python likely fine. But we also need combine with -inf. Could implement iterative segment tree for speed? Need store tuple of 5 values per node; updating leaf and recomputing ancestors. Iterative segment tree size power of two. Each update recomputes ~log n nodes, each combine uses tuples. 2e5*17=3.4M combines, fine. Recursive maybe also fine. But final code should be robust.

Need ensure best1 for node with cnt=0 remains NEG, not maybe sum? Let's define combine carefully.

Leaf:
sum = val
cnt = alive (1/0)
pref = val if alive else NEG
suff = val if alive else NEG
best = val if alive else NEG

Build tree by pulling from size-1 down to 1:

left = i*2; right = i*2+1

# pref

pref[i] = p
# suff
s = suff[right]
v = sum[right] + suff[left]
if v > s: s = v
suff[i] = s
# best
b = best[left]
if best[right] > b: b = best[right]
v = suff[left] + pref[right]
if v > b: b = v
best[i] = b

Now, update function iterative:
def set_leaf(pos, val, alive):
    idx = pos + size
    sum[idx] = val
    cnt[idx] = 1 if alive else 0
    if alive:
        pref[idx] = suff[idx] = best[idx] = val
    else:
        pref[idx] = suff[idx] = best[idx] = NEG
    idx >>= 1
    while idx:
        pull(idx)
        idx >>= 1

We need update positions 0-indexed. For each v, for i in positions[v]: update(i, 0, False). Then query root best[1]. Then restore: update(i, nums[i], True).

We can skip if freq[v] == n? But update all to dead would set cnt=0, best=NEG; restore. We can just process, but if freq==n, after updates root.cnt=0, best=NEG; ans unchanged. But we still do 2n updates. Fine. Could optimize by not processing if freq[v]==n? Not necessary. But if n=1e5 and all same, updates 2e5, okay.

However, there is a subtle issue: We process each distinct value v by updating all occurrences to dead. But if there are duplicate values, after restoring previous value, tree back to original. Good.

We need maintain for each segment:
- sum: total value (dead 0)
- cnt: number of alive elements
- pref: maximum sum of a non-empty prefix that contains at least one alive. If none, NEG.
- suff: maximum sum of a non-empty suffix that contains at least one alive. If none, NEG.
- best: maximum sum of a non-empty subarray that contains at least one alive. If none, NEG.

Combine L,R:
Any prefix of combined with alive:
1. Prefix entirely within L and has alive: L.pref.
2. Prefix includes all of L and a non-empty prefix of R. It has alive iff L has alive or R prefix has alive. If L has alive, the sum is L.sum + (sum of prefix of R). To maximize, we can choose any prefix of R, possibly empty? But prefix of combined must be non-empty and include all L plus some prefix of R. If R prefix empty, then prefix is just all L, which is already covered by L.pref? Is L.pref >= L.sum if L has alive? Yes, because all L is a prefix with alive, so L.pref is max over all prefixes with alive, so >= L.sum. Thus we don't need empty R prefix. If R prefix non-empty and has alive, sum = L.sum + R.pref (max prefix with alive). If R prefix has no alive, sum = L.sum + 0 (dead prefix), which is <= L.pref as above if L has alive. If L has no alive, then prefix must include R prefix with alive; L.sum=0, sum = R.pref. This is covered by L.sum + R.pref. If L has no alive and R prefix no alive, invalid. So formula max(L.pref, L.sum + R.pref) works. Need ensure R.pref is max prefix with alive, not max prefix any. If L has alive and R has a dead-only prefix with sum 0, L.sum + 0 <= L.pref. If L has no alive, dead-only prefix invalid. So okay.

Suffix symmetric.

Any subarray with alive:
1. Entirely in L: L.best.
2. Entirely in R: R.best.

We claim max cross is L.suff + R.pref, where both have alive? Wait what if suffix of L has alive and prefix of R has no alive, cross sum = L.suff1 + 0. This is <= L.best? Let's design update function:

def update(pos, val, alive):
    idx = pos + size
    sumv[idx] = val
    cnt[idx] = 1 if alive else 0
    if alive:
        pref[idx] = suff[idx] = best[idx] = val
    else:
        pref[idx] = suff[idx] = best[idx] = NEG
    idx >>= 1
    while idx:
        l = idx << 1
        r = l | 1
        # sum
        sl = sumv[l]; sr = sumv[r]
        sumv[idx] = sl + sr
        cnt[idx] = cnt[l] + cnt[r]
        # pref
        p = pref[l]
        v = sl + pref[r]
        if v > p:
            p = v
        pref[idx] = p
        # suff
        s = suff[r]
        v = sr + suff[l]
        if v > s:
            s = v
        suff[idx] = s
        # best
        b = best[l]
        br = best[r]
        if br > b:
            b = br
        v = suff[l] + pref[r]
        if v > b:
            b = v
        best[idx] = b
        idx >>= 1

This avoids pull function calls. Build can use same code in a loop. We can define a `pull(i)` for build and update? To avoid duplication, maybe define `pull` and use in update; but for performance, inline in update. Build only size ~131k pulls, not huge. Could use a function for build, and inline update? Or define `pull` and use for both; 3.4M calls okay. But final code can be cleaner with `pull`. Let's decide.

Given desired oververbosity 5, final code only. We can include a nested `pull` function for readability. But if time limit strict, inline might be better. But is it absolutely true that deleting a positive value cannot improve? Let's prove more rigorously:
For any x > 0, consider any subarray S in the array after deleting x. It corresponds to a set of original elements not equal to x that are consecutive among non-x elements. Let I be the original interval from first to last element of S (if S non-empty). The sum of S equals sum of elements in I with value != x. The original sum of I = sum(S) + sum of x elements in I. Since x>0, original sum >= sum(S). Thus every subarray after deleting x has sum <= original sum of some interval I, which is <= original max subarray sum. Therefore g(x) <= original max. For x=0, original sum = sum(S), so g(0)=original max? Actually if x=0, original sum of I = sum(S) + 0 = sum(S), so g(0) <= original max, and original max can be achieved by no deletion, so g(0)=original max. Thus only x<0 can improve. Good.

So we can process only negative values. This also avoids the issue of deleting positive values causing root.best1 maybe invalid? Not needed. But if we process only negative, we don't need to consider x=0 deletion, which yields original max. Good.

Now, let's think about if we should use `nonlocal` in nested functions? We can avoid nested functions by inlining. Let's write final code with a nested `pull` for clarity? The final answer code can be:

```python
from typing import List
from collections import defaultdict

class Solution:
    def maxSubarraySum(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        NEG = -10**30
        
        size = 1
        while size < n:
            size <<= 1
        
        sumv = [0] * (2 * size)
        cnt = [0] * (2 * size)
        pref = [NEG] * (2 * size)
        suff = [NEG] * (2 * size)
        best = [NEG] * (2 * size)
        
        for i, val in enumerate(nums):
            p = size + i
            sumv[p] = val
            cnt[p] = 1
            pref[p] = suff[p] = best[p] = val
        
        def pull(i: int) -> None:
            l = i << 1
            r = l | 1
            sl = sumv[l]
            sr = sumv[r]
            sumv[i] = sl + sr
            cnt[i] = cnt[l] + cnt[r]
            
            p = pref[l]
            v = sl + pref[r]
            if v > p:
                p = v
            pref[i] = p
            
            s = suff[r]
            v = sr + suff[l]
            if v > s:
                s = v
            suff[i] = s
            
            b = best[l]
            if best[r] > b:
                b = best[r]
            v = suff[l] + pref[r]
            if v > b:
                b = v
            best[i] = b
        
        for i in range(size - 1, 0, -1):
            pull(i)
        
        ans = best[1]
        
        pos = defaultdict(list)
        for i, val in enumerate(nums):
            if val < 0:
                pos[val].append(i)
        
        def update(idx: int, val: int, alive: int) -> None:
            p = size + idx
            sumv[p] = val
            cnt[p] = alive
            if alive:
                pref[p] = suff[p] = best[p] = val
            else:
                pref[p] = suff[p] = best[p] = NEG
            p >>= 1
            while p:
                pull(p)
                p >>= 1
        
        for v, lst in pos.items():
            if len(lst) == n:
                continue
            for idx in lst:
                update(idx, 0, 0)
            if cnt[1] > 0 and best[1] > ans:
                ans = best[1]
            for idx in lst:
                update(idx, nums[idx], 1)
        
        return ans
```

This uses nested `pull` and `update`; function calls: update 2 * total negative occurrences <=2e5, each calls pull ~17 -> 3.4M pull calls. Plus build pull size ~131k. 3.5M function calls. Actually `timeit` for empty function call: 10 million calls ~0.5s? Maybe. So 3.5M calls ~0.2s? Not bad. But pull does many operations, so total maybe 2-3s. LeetCode Python time limit maybe 2s? Could be tight but likely okay for 1e5. To be safer, we can inline pull in update and build to avoid 3.4M function calls. But code becomes longer. We can create an iterative update with inline combine:

```
def update(pos, val, alive):
    p = size + pos
    sumv[p] = val
    cnt[p] = alive
    if alive:
        pref[p] = suff[p] = best[p] = val
    else:
        pref[p] = suff[p] = best[p] = NEG
    p >>= 1
    while p:
        l = p << 1
        r = l | 1
        sl = sumv[l]; sr = sumv[r]
        sumv[p] = sl + sr
        cnt[p] = cnt[l] + cnt[r]
        
        x = pref[l]
        y = sl + pref[r]
        if y > x: x = y
        pref[p] = x
        
        x = suff[r]
        y = sr + suff[l]
        if y > x: x = y
        suff[p] = x
        
        x = best[l]
        if best[r] > x: x = best[r]
        y = suff[l] + pref[r]
        if y > x: x = y
        best[p] = x
        
        p >>= 1
```

This avoids pull function calls. Build can use the same code in a loop, but build only size ~131k, function call not big. We can define a separate `pull` for build or inline build with same code. The sum is sum of alive elements. So the optimal sum after deleting v is simply the maximum sum of a contiguous block of alive elements in the filtered array. Can we compute max subarray sum after deleting each negative v by considering the array split by v occurrences? For fixed v, the filtered array is concatenation of blocks of non-v elements. The max subarray sum of filtered array is max over:
- max subarray within a single block (standard)
- max sum of a suffix of block i + sum of full blocks i+1..j-1 + prefix of block j, for i<j.
This is like maximum subarray sum over an array where each block is a "super-element" with internal prefix/suffix? Actually if we compress each block into its sum? Wait, to span across multiple v's, we must include entire intermediate blocks, but endpoints can be suffix/prefix. This is equivalent to maximum subarray sum in an array where each block is represented by its total sum, but with the ability to choose suffix/prefix of endpoint blocks. More precisely, if we define block sums B_i, then max spanning multiple blocks is max over i<=j of (suffix_i if i=j? no) ... For i<j: suffix_i + sum_{k=i+1}^{j-1} B_k + prefix_j. This is exactly maximum subarray sum in the sequence of block sums if we allow choosing a suffix of first block and prefix of last block. If we replace each block by its total sum, then max subarray over block sums gives sum of full blocks, not suffix/prefix. But we can include suffix/prefix by considering within block max prefix/suffix. This is exactly what segment tree does.

Could process each value v by scanning its occurrences and using precomputed block prefix/suffix? For each v, the blocks are the segments between occurrences of v. We need max subarray sum of filtered array. This can be computed from:
- For each block, its max subarray, max prefix, max suffix, sum.
Then the filtered array is a sequence of blocks. The max subarray sum over the sequence of blocks (where each block is a subarray with prefix/suffix) can be computed by Kadane over blocks using block summaries. Specifically, if each block has sum, pref, suff, best, then the max subarray sum of concatenation of blocks is the same combine as segment tree but with blocks as leaves. For fixed v, we could compute this in O(number of blocks) = O(freq(v)+1) by scanning blocks. Sum over v of freq(v) = n, but sum over v of number of blocks = sum (freq(v)+1) = n + distinct. That is O(n)! Wait, this might yield an O(n) algorithm! Let's explore.

For each distinct value v, the array after deleting v is the concatenation of blocks of elements not equal to v. The blocks are separated by occurrences of v. If we can precompute for each block its summary (sum, pref, suff, best) quickly, then for each v, we can scan the blocks in order and combine their summaries to get the max subarray sum of the filtered array. The total number of blocks over all v is sum_v (freq(v)+1) = n + d, where d is number of distinct values. Because each occurrence of v is a separator; for a fixed v, there are freq(v)+1 blocks. Summing over v gives n + d. If we can get each block summary in O(1), then total O(n). This is much better! Let's investigate.

For fixed v, the blocks are maximal contiguous segments of original array that do not contain v. We need for each block:
- sum: sum of elements in block
- pref: max prefix sum of block (non-empty)
- suff: max suffix sum
- best: max subarray sum within block
Then the max subarray sum of the concatenation of these blocks (which is the filtered array) can be computed by combining block summaries in order using the same combine as segment tree, but with each block as a leaf (all elements alive, no dead). Actually filtered array is exactly concatenation of blocks (non-v elements) with no gaps. So we can compute max subarray sum by Kadane/combine over block summaries. If we scan blocks in order, maintain combined summary of previous blocks, and combine with current block summary. The final best is g(v). This would be O(number of blocks) per v, total O(n + d). Need compute block summaries for each v efficiently.

Can we precompute block summaries for all v? For each v, blocks are intervals between consecutive occurrences of v (and array boundaries). We need summary of an arbitrary interval [L,R] that contains no v. Since v is absent from the block by definition. The block is a contiguous segment of original array with no occurrence of v. We need its summary (sum, pref, suff, best). This is a range query on the original array for any interval. We can build a segment tree/sparse table for range max subarray summary to answer range summary in O(log n) per block. Then total O((n+d) log n), which is O(n log n) but maybe fewer updates? Actually number of blocks n+d up to 2e5, range query O(log n) -> 3.4M, similar. But we can maybe precompute block summaries in O(1) using prefix sums and prefix/suffix? Need max subarray in arbitrary interval without v. Range max subarray query can be answered with segment tree O(log n). That's same complexity but maybe fewer updates? Let's see: For each v, number of blocks = freq(v)+1. Sum = n+d. If d up to n, total blocks ~2n. Range query O(log n) -> 3.4M, similar to update approach but no updates, just queries. But we also need combine blocks per v; total combines over blocks ~n+d. Range query returns a Node summary; combining blocks uses same combine. This might be faster than updates? It avoids 2n updates but does n+d range queries. Range query on segment tree O(log n) with more overhead than point update? Similar. But maybe we can do O(1) range summary using a disjoint sparse table? Since range max subarray query is associative and can be answered with a segment tree in O(log n), or with a sparse table in O(1) if the operation is associative and idempotent? Max subarray combine is associative but not idempotent? It is associative; we can use sparse table for range queries if operation is associative and we can combine overlapping ranges? For range maximum subarray, the combine operation is associative but not idempotent, so sparse table with overlapping intervals doesn't work (because combining same interval twice changes sum). Disjoint sparse table can answer associative range queries in O(1) with O(n log n) preprocessing. That might be overkill.

But maybe we can compute block summaries for all v in O(n) using a different technique: For each value v, blocks are intervals between occurrences. We can precompute for every position the nearest occurrence? Hmm.

Let's explore the block-scanning approach more. It might lead to O(n log n) with range query segment tree, but perhaps we can avoid range queries by computing block summaries incrementally.

For fixed v, as we scan the original array, blocks are segments between v occurrences. The summary of each block can be computed by scanning the block. If we naively scan all blocks for all v, total elements scanned over all v = sum_v (n - freq(v)) = n*d - n, too large. Need precompute.

But note: A block for v is an interval that contains no v. It may contain other values. We need its summary. This is exactly a range query on original array. We can use a segment tree for range summary. Then total range queries = n+d. Each query O(log n). This is O(n log n) but with no updates. Maybe simpler? Let's compare with update approach:
- Update approach: build segment tree once, for each negative v, update all occurrences to dead (k updates), query root O(1), restore (k updates). Total updates 2 * total_neg_occurrences <=2n, each O(log n). No range queries.
- Block approach: build segment tree for range summary, for each negative v, get blocks by occurrences, query each block summary O(log n), combine. Total queries = total blocks over processed v = n_neg + d_neg <=2n. Each O(log n). Similar. But block approach doesn't need updates/restores, and can maybe process only negative values. It might be more intuitive? Let's verify correctness.

For fixed v, filtered array = concatenation of blocks B0, B1, ..., Bk where k=freq(v), with B0 before first v, B_i between v_i and v_{i+1}, B_k after last v. Some blocks may be empty if v occurrences adjacent. Empty blocks should be ignored. The max subarray sum of filtered array is the max subarray sum of the concatenation of non-empty blocks. We can combine block summaries in order. If all blocks empty (freq==n), no valid array; skip. If some blocks non-empty, combine.

We need a data structure to get summary of an interval [L,R] inclusive in original array. A segment tree can return a Node {sum, pref, suff, best} for any range. Since all elements in the block are alive (not v), the summary is standard max subarray summary (non-empty). For empty interval, summary should be identity? For combining blocks, empty blocks can be skipped. We can query only non-empty blocks. How to get blocks for v efficiently? We have positions list for v. Blocks:
- before first: [0, pos[0]-1]
- between pos[i]+1 and pos[i+1]-1
- after last: [pos[-1]+1, n-1]
If start > end, empty skip.
For each non-empty block, query range summary, combine into accumulator.

Total blocks over all v = sum (freq(v)+1) = n + d. For each v, if freq(v)==n, there is one empty block? Actually blocks: before first empty, between empty, after last empty? If all positions v, blocks all empty. We skip. If freq<n, at least one non-empty block. We can process.

This approach uses a segment tree for range queries. We need implement range query returning Node. The combine operation is same as before but without alive/dead (all alive). Node fields: sum, pref, suff, best. For a leaf val: sum=pref=suff=best=val. For empty query, return identity? We can avoid empty queries. But for combine accumulator, we need an identity Node for no blocks. What is identity for combine? Combining with identity should return the other Node. For standard max subarray summary, identity for empty segment: sum=0, pref=suff=best = NEG? Let's see combine(identity, R):
sum = 0 + R.sum = R.sum
pref = max(identity.pref=NEG, identity.sum + R.pref = R.pref) = R.pref
suff = max(R.suff, R.sum + identity.suff=NEG) = R.suff
best = max(NEG, R.best, identity.suff + R.pref = NEG) = R.best. Good. So identity is same as empty/dead node: sum=0, pref=suff=best=NEG. We can use that.

Range query on segment tree: We need combine nodes in correct order. Iterative segment tree range query can return left accumulator and right accumulator. Since combine is associative but not commutative, need maintain order. Standard:
```
def query(l, r): # inclusive 0-index
    l += size; r += size
    left_res = IDENTITY
    right_res = IDENTITY
    while l <= r:
        if l & 1:
            left_res = combine(left_res, tree[l])
            l += 1
        if not (r & 1):
            right_res = combine(tree[r], right_res)
            r -= 1
        l >>= 1; r >>= 1
    return combine(left_res, right_res)
```
Need tree nodes for leaves beyond n as identity. Build tree with identity for empty leaves. For leaves within n, node val. Internal nodes combine children. This is similar to previous segment tree but no cnt. Actually previous tree with cnt all alive and extra leaves identity is exactly this. We can reuse.

Then for each negative v:
```
acc = IDENTITY
prev = 0? 
positions = pos[v]
start = 0
for p in positions:
    if start <= p-1:
        node = query(start, p-1)
        acc = combine(acc, node)
    start = p+1
if start <= n-1:
    node = query(start, n-1)
    acc = combine(acc, node)
if acc.best != NEG: ans = max(ans, acc.best)
```
But wait, if v has freq==n, positions cover all, start=0, for p in positions: start<=p-1? first p=0, start=0, p-1=-1 false; start=1; next p=1, start=1, p-1=0 false; ... after last start=n, start<=n-1 false. acc identity, best=NEG. Skip. If freq<n, acc.best valid.

Total range queries = number of non-empty blocks. In worst-case, for each v, blocks count freq+1, but some empty. Sum of non-empty blocks <= n+d. Actually if v occurrences adjacent, empty blocks reduce count. So <= n+d. For n=1e5, ~2e5 queries. Each query O(log n) ~17 combines. 3.4M combines. Similar to update approach but no updates. However, range query combine uses tree nodes and accumulators; each query does ~2*log combines? Standard iterative query combines when l odd/r even, up to 2log nodes, plus final combine. So ~34 combines per query. 2e5 queries -> 6.8M combines, more than update approach's 3.4M pulls. But no updates. Maybe similar. We can optimize range query by using a recursive? Not.

Can we get block summaries in O(1) using prefix/suffix arrays per value? Let's think.

For a fixed v, blocks are intervals between occurrences of v. The summary of an interval [L,R] can be computed if we have:
- sum: prefix sum O(1)
- pref: max prefix sum in [L,R] = max_{k in [L,R]} (P[k] - P[L-1]) = max_prefix_sum_until? This is range maximum of prefix sums. Can answer O(1) with sparse table.
- suff: max suffix sum in [L,R] = max_{k in [L,R]} (P[R] - P[k-1]) = P[R] - min_{k in [L,R]} P[k-1]. Range minimum of prefix sums O(1) with sparse table.
- best: max subarray sum in [L,R] = max_{l<=r} P[r]-P[l-1]. This is range maximum subarray sum, which cannot be answered with simple prefix min/max? Actually max subarray sum in interval can be computed from prefix sums: max_{i<j} P[j]-P[i]. This is not just range min/max of P because i<j. Need a segment tree/sparse table. But maybe we can precompute best for intervals between occurrences? Not trivial.

Could use a Cartesian tree? Not needed.

Another idea: Since we only need max over v, maybe we can compute answer by considering each negative value's occurrences and using Kadane on the fly with prefix sums? Hmm.

Let's explore if there is an O(n) algorithm using "maximum subarray with one color removed" known. Maybe we can compute for each value v the maximum subarray sum after deleting v using a single pass and maintaining something.

Let's derive from D_v prefix. For each v, g(v) = max_{i<j, at least one non-v} D_v[j] - D_v[i]. D_v changes only at non-v positions. We need max over v. This is like for each pair (i,j), value = P[j]-P[i] - (Q_v[j]-Q_v[i]). For a fixed pair, the best v to delete is the value with minimum sum in interval (negative). The answer = max over intervals [i+1..j] of P[j]-P[i] - min(0, min_v S_v). This is reminiscent of "maximum subarray sum after removing one element" where you keep track of minimum prefix sum of each value. Could use divide and conquer over indices: For intervals crossing mid, compute best deletion value? Let's see.

Divide and conquer: For each segment, compute max subarray sum after deleting one value? The answer over intervals crossing mid can be computed by considering left suffix and right prefix. For a crossing interval, total sum = suffix_sum_left + prefix_sum_right. The contribution of a value v is S_v_left + S_v_right. We need subtract min(0, min_v S_v). We could compute for each value the minimum contribution in left suffix + right prefix. This seems complex but maybe manageable with maps.

Segment tree update approach is simpler and likely accepted.

Let's think about the block-scanning approach with range query segment tree. It might be more elegant and avoids mutating tree. But update approach is easier to reason. Which is faster? Let's compare code complexity.

Update approach:
- Build tree with alive flags.
- For each negative v, update occurrences to dead, query root, restore.
- Total updates 2 * total_neg_occurrences. If all negative distinct, 2n updates. Each update recomputes ancestors from leaf to root: log n combines. 2n log n = 3.4M combines for n=1e5? Wait 2n = 2e5, log=17, 3.4M. Yes.
- Query root O(1).
- No range queries.

Block approach:
- Build tree for range summary (same combine).
- For each negative v, for each block query range. Total blocks <= n + d_neg. If all negative distinct, d_neg=n, each value has freq=1, blocks per value = 2 (before and after) but one may be empty depending position. Total non-empty blocks ~ n + d? For distinct values, each value appears once, blocks: before and after. Sum over values of non-empty blocks = for each occurrence, left block (before) and right block (after). If positions distributed, total blocks = 2n? Let's compute: For each value with one occurrence at index p, blocks [0,p-1] and [p+1,n-1]. Sum of lengths over all values? Non-empty blocks count: for each occurrence, left block non-empty if p>0, right non-empty if p<n-1. So total non-empty blocks <=2n. Range queries 2n, each O(log n) with ~2log combines => 6.8M combines. More than update approach. But if values have higher freq, blocks fewer. Worst-case distinct gives 2n queries. Update approach worst-case distinct gives 2n updates. Each update combine log, each range query combine ~2log. So update approach likely faster.

Can we optimize block approach by querying blocks with a segment tree that returns Node in O(log n) but with fewer combines? Standard iterative query combines up to 2log nodes, but average maybe log. Still more than update. So update approach better.

Could we process block approach without range query by precomputing block summaries using a "next occurrence" and prefix/suffix? Not worth.

Thus segment tree update approach is good.

Now, let's think about if we can reduce update approach's updates by processing only negative values and skipping freq==n. Worst-case all negative distinct -> 2n updates. More precisely, if no positive element, original max negative and deleting negative doesn't improve. But checking requires more. Not needed.

Now, let's think about if we can reduce updates by using a segment tree where updating a leaf to dead and restoring can be combined? No.

Pseudo final:

```python
from typing import List
from collections import defaultdict

class Solution:
    def maxSubarraySum(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        NEG = -10**30
        size = 1
        while size < n:
            size <<= 1
        
        sumv = [0]*(2*size)
        cnt = [0]*(2*size)
        pref = [NEG]*(2*size)
        suff = [NEG]*(2*size)
        best = [NEG]*(2*size)
        
        for i, val in enumerate(nums):
            p = size + i
            sumv[p] = val
            cnt[p] = 1
            pref[p] = val
            suff[p] = val
            best[p] = val
        
        # build
        for i in range(size-1, 0, -1):
            l = i<<1; r = l|1
            sl = sumv[l]; sr = sumv[r]
            sumv[i] = sl + sr
            cnt[i] = cnt[l] + cnt[r]
            
            x = pref[l]
            y = sl + pref[r]
            if y > x: x = y
            pref[i] = x
            
            x = suff[r]
            y = sr + suff[l]
            if y > x: x = y
            suff[i] = x
            
            x = best[l]
            if best[r] > x: x = best[r]
            y = suff[l] + pref[r]
            if y > x: x = y
            best[i] = x
        
        ans = best[1]
        
        pos = defaultdict(list)
        for i, val in enumerate(nums):
            if val < 0:
                pos[val].append(i)
        
        def update(idx: int, val: int, alive: int) -> None:
            p = size + idx
            sumv[p] = val
            cnt[p] = alive
            if alive:
                pref[p] = val
                suff[p] = val
                best[p] = val
            else:
                pref[p] = NEG
                suff[p] = NEG
                best[p] = NEG
            p >>= 1
            while p:
                l = p << 1
                r = l | 1
                sl = sumv[l]
                sr = sumv[r]
                sumv[p] = sl + sr
                cnt[p] = cnt[l] + cnt[r]
                
                x = pref[l]
                y = sl + pref[r]
                if y > x:
                    x = y
                pref[p] = x
                
                x = suff[r]
                y = sr + suff[l]
                if y > x:
                    x = y
                suff[p] = x
                
                x = best[l]
                if best[r] > x:
                    x = best[r]
                y = suff[l] + pref[r]
                if y > x:
                    x = y
                best[p] = x
                
                p >>= 1
        
        for v, lst in pos.items():
            if len(lst) == n:
                continue
            for idx in lst:
                update(idx, 0, 0)
            if cnt[1] > 0 and best[1] > ans:
                ans = best[1]
            for idx in lst:
                update(idx, nums[idx], 1)
        
        return ans
```

Need ensure `alive` parameter type int; in Python, `cnt[p] = alive` where alive is 0 or 1. Good.

Potential issue: In build, for extra leaves, cnt=0, pref/suff/best=NEG, sum=0. For internal nodes, combine works. Good.

Potential issue: In update, when restoring a leaf to original value, if original value is negative, pref/suff/best=val. Good.

nums = [-1, 2, -1, 3, -1]. Original max? [2,-1,3]=4, [2]=2, [3]=3, max4. Delete -1 -> [2,3] sum5. Segment tree v=-1: [0,2,0,3,0], best1=5. ans5. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with using `cnt` in combine but not in `pref`/`suff`/`best` formulas. We rely on cnt only for root check and leaf setting. The combine formulas don't use cnt except sum? Actually pref/suff/best formulas don't need cnt if invalid sentinels are correct. cnt is used to know if node has alive, but sentinels encode that. - sum = sl+sr
- pref = max(pref[l], sl + pref[r])
- suff = max(suff[r], sr + suff[l])
- best = max(best[l], best[r], suff[l] + pref[r])
This is exactly the standard segment tree for max subarray with empty/dead leaves as identity. It doesn't need cnt. Does it correctly handle a segment with alive zeros? Yes, leaf alive 0 has pref/suff/best=0, sum=0. A segment with only alive zeros has best=0. A segment with no alive has best=NEG. Good.

So we can remove cnt. This simplifies and speeds up. Let's test with previous examples:

But note: If we define update with default arguments, and later modify arrays, default references see modifications. Good.

Let's write update with default args:
```
def update(idx, val, alive, sumv=sumv, pref=pref, suff=suff, best=best, size=size, NEG=NEG):
    p = size + idx
    sumv[p] = val
    if alive:
        pref[p] = suff[p] = best[p] = val
    else:
        pref[p] = suff[p] = best[p] = NEG
    p >>= 1
    while p:
        l = p << 1
        r = l | 1
        sl = sumv[l]
        sr = sumv[r]
        sumv[p] = sl + sr
        x = pref[l]
        y = sl + pref[r]
        if y > x:
            x = y
        pref[p] = x
        x = suff[r]
        y = sr + suff[l]
        if y > x:
            x = y
        suff[p] = x
        x = best[l]
        if best[r] > x:
            x = best[r]
        y = suff[l] + pref[r]
        if y > x:
            x = y
        best[p] = x
        p >>= 1
```
This is good.

Could also inline update in processing loop to avoid function call overhead, but update function call 2e5 times, not huge. Inside update, while loop 17 iterations. Function call overhead 2e5 ~ maybe 0.02s? Actually 2e5 function calls ~0.02-0.1s. Fine.

Before final, let's think if there is any hidden nuance: The operation removes all occurrences of x from the array. If x appears, after deletion, the array's elements shift. Our transformed array with zeros at x positions preserves original length. We argued equivalence. Let's prove more formally:
Let F be array after removing all x. For any subarray of F, let its elements correspond to original indices i1 < i2 < ... < im, all not x, and consecutive in F means there is no non-x element between i1 and im outside this set. Thus the original interval [i1, im] contains exactly these non-x elements plus some x's. In transformed array T (x->0), the subarray from i1 to im has sum equal to sum of non-x elements in interval, which is the F subarray sum, and contains at least one non-x. Conversely, any subarray of T with at least one non-x, from l to r, has non-x elements inside; in F they form a contiguous block because any non-x element between l and r is inside the interval. Its sum equals T subarray sum. Thus max equal. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with the problem's constraints and the use of O(n log n). Should be accepted. But let's see if there is an even simpler O(n) solution that the problem might expect, and if our O(n log n) might TLE in Python. Let's explore further to be confident.

Could we compute answer by Kadane with "delete all occurrences of one value" using a segment tree of prefix minima per value? Let's think.

We want max over v of max_{i<j} D_v[j]-D_v[i]. For each v, D_v[i] = P[i] - Q_v[i]. We can process indices left to right, maintaining for each value v the minimum D_v[i] seen so far. Then for current j, g(v) candidate = D_v[j] - min_{i<j} D_v[i]. But D_v[j] depends on v: if a_j = v, D_v[j] = D_v[j-1] (since v contributes 0); else D_v[j] = D_v[j-1] + a_j. This is like maintaining D_v for all v as we scan. There are n values, updating all D_v per step impossible. But maybe we can maintain only values that appear? Let's examine.

For a fixed v, D_v changes only at positions where a_i != v. At position j with a_j = w:
- For v = w: D_w[j] = D_w[j-1] (no change)
- For v != w: D_v[j] = D_v[j-1] + w (all other values add w)
So at each step, one value's D stays constant, all others increase by w. This is a global offset plus per-value adjustments. We can maintain a global add offset and per-value base. Let D_v[j] = base_v + offset_j maybe. At step j, for v != w, D_v increases by w; for v=w, no increase. So offset_j = sum of elements up to j? Actually if we define D_v[j] = P[j] - Q_v[j]. P[j] is global. Q_v[j] changes only when a_j=v. So D_v[j] = P[j] - Q_v[j]. For fixed j, P[j] common, Q_v[j] is sum of occurrences of v up to j. We need min over i<j of P[i] - Q_v[i]. This is P[i] minus prefix sum of v. Could maintain for each v the minimum of P[i] - Q_v[i] over i<j. When we move j, for all v except a_j, Q_v unchanged, so the minimum for those v just gets new candidate P[j] - Q_v[j] = P[j] - Q_v[j-1]. For v=a_j, Q_v increases by a_j (negative), so D_v[j] = P[j] - Q_v[j] = P[j] - (Q_v[j-1]+a_j) = D_v[j-1] (since P[j]=P[j-1]+a_j). So D_v doesn't change. This is complex but maybe maintainable with lazy global additions and per-value exceptions. For each v, min_{i<j} D_v[i] = min over i of P[i] - Q_v[i]. At step j, for all v != w, the new candidate is P[j] - Q_v[j] = P[j] - Q_v[j-1]. Since P[j] is global, and Q_v[j-1] is per-value. For v=w, candidate is same as previous D_w[j-1]? Actually D_w[j] = D_w[j-1], so the minimum up to j for w is min(previous min, D_w[j]) = previous min (since D_w[j] = D_w[j-1] already included). So no new candidate for w. For all v != w, we add a new candidate with value P[j] - Q_v[j-1]. This is like for each v, the sequence of candidate D_v at positions where a_i != v. Hard.

Maybe we can maintain a segment tree over values with lazy updates? At each index j with value w:
- For all v != w, D_v[j] = D_v[j-1] + w, so the "current D" for v increases by w.
- For v=w, D_w[j] = D_w[j-1].
We want for each v the minimum D_v over time. This is analogous to maintaining n sequences with range add on all except one, and point query min. Could use segment tree over values with lazy "add to all except value w" = add to all, subtract from w. Then at each step, update current D_v, and update min_v = min(min_v, current D_v). Then answer for v at position j? Actually g(v) = max_j (D_v[j] - min_{i<j} D_v[i]). We could maintain current D_v and min D_v for each v, and update answer for v when current D_v - min_v is max. At each index j, for all v != w, current D_v increases by w; for v=w, current D_w unchanged. Then for each v, candidate = current D_v - min_v. But updating all v per index is too much. However, with segment tree over values supporting:
- range add to all values except w (i.e., add w to all, add -w to w)
- update min_v = min(min_v, current D_v) for all v? Actually min_v is the minimum of current D_v over time. At each step, after updating current D_v, we need to set min_v = min(min_v, current D_v) for all v. This is a pointwise chmin of min_v with current D_v. Segment tree can maintain current D_v and min_v, but chmin for all v is hard. Maybe we don't need min_v for all v? We need max over v of current D_v - min_v. This is like maintaining for each v the best difference. Could be done with segment tree beats? Too complex.

So segment tree update approach is reasonable.

Another possible O(n log n) approach: Use divide and conquer over values? Not needed.

LeetCode time limit maybe 2s? But PyPy might be faster. The problem might be from LeetCode weekly contest; Python solutions often use O(n log n) and pass.
```
def kadane(nums):
    cur = nums[0]; best = nums[0]
    for x in nums[1:]:
        cur = max(x, cur + x)
        best = max(best, cur)
    return best
ans = kadane(nums)
if ans <= 0: return ans
# build segment tree...
```
This avoids building segment tree for all-non-positive arrays. But we still need segment tree for processing if ans>0. </think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List
from collections import defaultdict

class Solution:
    def maxSubarraySum(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        NEG = -10**30

        size = 1
        while size < n:
            size <<= 1

        sumv = [0] * (2 * size)
        pref = [NEG] * (2 * size)
        suff = [NEG] * (2 * size)
        best = [NEG] * (2 * size)

        for i, val in enumerate(nums):
            p = size + i
            sumv[p] = val
            pref[p] = suff[p] = best[p] = val

        for i in range(size - 1, 0, -1):
            l = i << 1
            r = l | 1
            sl = sumv[l]
            sr = sumv[r]
            sumv[i] = sl + sr

            x = pref[l]
            y = sl + pref[r]
            if y > x:
                x = y
            pref[i] = x

            x = suff[r]
            y = sr + suff[l]
            if y > x:
                x = y
            suff[i] = x

            x = best[l]
            if best[r] > x:
                x = best[r]
            y = suff[l] + pref[r]
            if y > x:
                x = y
            best[i] = x

        ans = best[1]

        # If the original maximum subarray sum is <= 0, deleting a value
        # cannot improve it.
        if ans <= 0:
            return ans

        pos = defaultdict(list)
        for i, val in enumerate(nums):
            if val < 0:
                pos[val].append(i)

        def update(idx: int, val: int, alive: int,
                   sumv=sumv, pref=pref, suff=suff, best=best,
                   size=size, NEG=NEG) -> None:
            p = size + idx
            sumv[p] = val
            if alive:
                pref[p] = suff[p] = best[p] = val
            else:
                pref[p] = suff[p] = best[p] = NEG

            p >>= 1
            while p:
                l = p << 1
                r = l | 1
                sl = sumv[l]
                sr = sumv[r]
                sumv[p] = sl + sr

                x = pref[l]
                y = sl + pref[r]
                if y > x:
                    x = y
                pref[p] = x

                x = suff[r]
                y = sr + suff[l]
                if y > x:
                    x = y
                suff[p] = x

                x = best[l]
                if best[r] > x:
                    x = best[r]
                y = suff[l] + pref[r]
                if y > x:
                    x = y
                best[p] = x

                p >>= 1

        for v, lst in pos.items():
            if len(lst) == n:
                continue

            for idx in lst:
                update(idx, 0, 0)

            if best[1] > ans:
                ans = best[1]

            for idx in lst:
                update(idx, nums[idx], 1)

        return ans
```
